I’ll look around because I think someone must be there, or maybe someone’s on the intercom, but the unit will be quiet, and nobody else reacts or hears anything. That’s what’s scaring me, because it feels so real in the moment, and then when I ask or check, it’s like… there’s no one there.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you hear ever tell you to do something or criticize you?\n\nPatient: It’s not really like they’re saying, “Go do this” or yelling insults at me, no. It’s more… they’re telling me things, like information I shouldn’t be hearing — which patient records have been changed, what was altered, that kind of thing. And it comes through the call-light speaker or sometimes the hallway vents, and it’s so clear that I’ll stop what I’m doing because it sounds like someone is right there beside me.\n\nBut then the unit is quiet, nobody else looks up, nobody reacts, and I’m standing there thinking, “How am I the only one hearing this?” So it doesn’t feel like criticism exactly, and not commands exactly. It feels like these voices are reporting something to me, and I can’t tell why I’m hearing it or where it’s actually coming from.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious, depressed, or irritable when you're not hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not depressed so much, at least not in the way people usually mean, but anxious and irritated, yes. Some days it’s like I wake up and I feel… switched on, like I’m finally awake and I can see what needs to be fixed at work. Like things are obvious to me and everyone else is moving too slowly or pretending not to notice.\n\nAnd then if my husband says, “Maybe you should rest,” or one of the nurses tells me to slow down or just do one thing at a time, I get really sharp with them. I snap before I even think about it, because in that moment it feels like they’re blocking something important, like they’re getting in the way of me handling what needs to be handled. Then later I can hear how I sounded, and I don’t like that, but at the time it feels urgent. It feels like I’m the only one taking it seriously.\n\nDoctor: Do you have periods where you feel extremely happy or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Extremely happy… I don’t know if I’d call it happy exactly. It’s more like I get this feeling that I’m finally awake, like my brain is turned on and I can see everything clearly, especially at work. Like I’m meant to fix things, or notice things other people are missing.\n\nOn those days I feel very driven, and it can feel good at first, like I have a purpose. But it turns irritable really fast. If my husband says I need to slow down, or if one of the nurses tells me to take a breath or focus on one thing, I snap at them. I hear myself getting sharp, like, “Why are you getting in my way?” because in that moment it feels like they’re blocking something important.\n\nSo… euphoric maybe isn’t the word I would use. It’s more like energized and sure of myself, like I’m supposed to handle everything, and then I get angry when people try to slow me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice a drop in your energy levels after these periods where you feel driven and like you can handle everything?\n\nPatient: Not right away, no. That’s the thing that scares my husband, I think, because after a full shift — and it’s not like those shifts are easy, I’m on my feet, answering call lights, moving patients, running around — I’ll come home and I still feel like there’s this motor in me.\n\nI’ll start cleaning the kitchen, then I’ll notice the counters need doing, then I’m rewriting notes or sorting through supplies I brought home or things I think need to be organized. And I’ll just keep going. I’ll pace around the kitchen while I’m talking, like I can’t just stand still, and my husband will be sitting there saying, “Please, just sit down for a minute,” or “You’ve already worked all day,” and I know he’s tired just watching me.\n\nBut in that moment I don’t feel tired. I feel like if I sit down, something important won’t get finished. It’s only later that I might realize I’ve been moving nonstop, but during it, no — it doesn’t feel like a drop. It feels like I have too much energy and nowhere for it to go.\n\nDoctor: Do these periods of feeling driven and energized come and go, or are they pretty much always there?\n\nPatient: They come and go, I think… but it’s been more like waves inside the same stretch of time, if that makes sense. This hasn’t been my whole life or anything. It’s been going on for about two months now.\n\nIt really seemed to start after I was asked to help lead the wound-care documentation rollout at work. Once that happened, it was like something clicked on in me. Some days it’s stronger — I feel like I have to keep pushing and fixing and organizing everything — and then other days it’s not as intense, but it still feels like it’s been hanging around since then. So not always there in the exact same way, but for these past couple months, it keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever believe things that might seem strange or unlikely to others, like conspiracy theories or thoughts that you're being followed or plotted against?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t call it a conspiracy theory exactly, because that makes it sound like I’m just making something up or sitting around imagining things. But yes, I do think there’s something going on with the wound-care charts.\n\nI’ve been looking at them so closely because of this rollout, and there’s a pattern there. I know there is. The way certain entries are late, or changed, or coded differently… it’s not random. It points to something management doesn’t want people to see. And when I’ve tried to bring it up, my supervisor keeps saying it’s just late documentation or coding mistakes, like I’m overthinking it.\n\nBut I can tell they’re watching what I write now. Not in a dramatic way like someone following me in a car or anything, but at work — the notes I make, the corrections I flag, who I talk to about it. It feels very clear to me that they know I caught something, and now they’re paying attention because they don’t want it coming out. So… I know how that might sound, but to me it doesn’t feel strange. It feels like I’m the only one actually seeing the pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the voices or the information they provide are specifically meant for you or about you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, that’s part of why I’m here, because it doesn’t feel like random noise or like I’m just overhearing something that has nothing to do with me. It feels directed at me.\n\nIt happens with the call-light speaker sometimes, or from the hallway vents. I know that sounds odd when I say it out loud, but it’s clear, like somebody standing right next to me and saying it where I can hear it. Not muffled like normal hallway noise. It’ll tell me which patient records have been changed, or which chart I need to look at again, and it feels like that information is being given to me specifically because I’m the one who’s supposed to catch it.\n\nAnd it’s not just when the unit is busy and there are people talking everywhere. Sometimes it’s quiet, or quiet enough that I know there isn’t someone there saying it, and nobody else reacts. Nobody turns their head, nobody answers, nobody says, “Did you hear that?” So then I’m standing there thinking, how am I the only one hearing this if it’s coming through so clearly? But it still feels meant for me, like it’s about what I’m finding in the records.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voices you're hearing could be your own mind trying to tell you something, rather than actual external voices?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to think of it that way, honestly. I’ve stood there and asked myself, “Is this just me? Is my brain trying to warn me because I’m stressed or because I’ve been looking at these charts too much?” I know that’s probably the reasonable explanation you’re trying to get at.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like my own thoughts. My thoughts feel like they’re inside my head, if that makes sense. This is different. It sounds like it’s coming from outside me — like from the call-light speaker or from the hallway vents — and it’s clear, not like a vague feeling or an intuition. It’ll be like someone standing right next to me telling me which patient record was changed or which chart I need to look at again.\n\nAnd the part that really scares me is it happens even when the unit is quiet enough that I can tell nobody is there saying it. Nobody else reacts, nobody looks up, nobody says they heard anything. So I can understand why you’d ask if it could be my mind, but in the moment it feels external. It feels like an actual voice coming through to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the information the voices are giving you about patient records being changed might not be true?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know you’re asking if maybe the voices are wrong, or if maybe I’m putting too much weight on what they say. I can understand why you’d ask that. But the reason it’s hard for me to say, “Yes, it might not be true,” is because it matches what I’m already seeing in the wound-care charts.\n\nIt’s not like the information is coming out of nowhere. I’ve been looking at those charts over and over, and there are patterns — late entries, things changed, codes that don’t line up the way they should. And every time I try to bring it up, my supervisor just brushes it off like it’s normal documentation delay or somebody made a coding mistake. But it doesn’t feel like mistakes. It feels too organized, like there’s something there management doesn’t want anyone to notice.\n\nAnd since I’m the one who caught it, I can tell they’re paying attention to me now. What I write down, what I flag, who I talk to about it — it feels like they’re watching that because they know I found the pattern. So… could I say it’s possible the voices are giving me wrong information? I guess anything is “possible” in the way people say that. But honestly, no, I don’t really believe that. To me it feels like the voices are pointing me toward what’s already there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have any doubts about the accuracy of the information the voices are giving you?\n\nPatient: I have doubts for a second, maybe, because I know how it sounds when I say it out loud. I know other people don’t hear it, and I know my supervisor keeps saying it’s just late entries or coding mistakes, like it’s nothing. So yes, there’s a part of me that tries to stop and ask, “Am I getting this wrong?”\n\nBut then I go back to the wound-care charts, and the pattern is still there. It’s not just one little mistake. It’s the way things are entered, the timing, what gets changed, what doesn’t match. And the voices aren’t telling me something totally separate from that — they’re pointing me back to what I’m already seeing.\n\nSo I don’t really doubt the information in the way you probably mean. I know those charts are showing something management doesn’t want anyone to see. And when my supervisor brushes it off, that almost makes me feel more sure, because it feels like she’s trying to make it seem ordinary when it isn’t.\n\nAnd I can tell they’re watching me now — what I write down, what I flag, who I mention it to — because I’m the one who caught it. So… I can say I try to question it, but deep down I think the voices are accurate. I think they’re telling me where to look because there really is something there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F23", "F25.1", "F31.2"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

It feels like an actual voice coming through to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the information the voices are giving you about patient records being changed might not be true?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know you’re asking if maybe the voices are wrong, or if maybe I’m putting too much weight on what they say. I can understand why you’d ask that. But the reason it’s hard for me to say, “Yes, it might not be true,” is because it matches what I’m already seeing in the wound-care charts.\n\nIt’s not like the information is coming out of nowhere. I’ve been looking at those charts over and over, and there are patterns — late entries, things changed, codes that don’t line up the way they should. And every time I try to bring it up, my supervisor just brushes it off like it’s normal documentation delay or somebody made a coding mistake. But it doesn’t feel like mistakes. It feels too organized, like there’s something there management doesn’t want anyone to notice.\n\nAnd since I’m the one who caught it, I can tell they’re paying attention to me now. What I write down, what I flag, who I talk to about it — it feels like they’re watching that because they know I found the pattern. So… could I say it’s possible the voices are giving me wrong information? I guess anything is “possible” in the way people say that. But honestly, no, I don’t really believe that. To me it feels like the voices are pointing me toward what’s already there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have any doubts about the accuracy of the information the voices are giving you?\n\nPatient: I have doubts for a second, maybe, because I know how it sounds when I say it out loud. I know other people don’t hear it, and I know my supervisor keeps saying it’s just late entries or coding mistakes, like it’s nothing. So yes, there’s a part of me that tries to stop and ask, “Am I getting this wrong?”\n\nBut then I go back to the wound-care charts, and the pattern is still there. It’s not just one little mistake. It’s the way things are entered, the timing, what gets changed, what doesn’t match. And the voices aren’t telling me something totally separate from that — they’re pointing me back to what I’m already seeing.\n\nSo I don’t really doubt the information in the way you probably mean. I know those charts are showing something management doesn’t want anyone to see. And when my supervisor brushes it off, that almost makes me feel more sure, because it feels like she’s trying to make it seem ordinary when it isn’t.\n\nAnd I can tell they’re watching me now — what I write down, what I flag, who I mention it to — because I’m the one who caught it. So… I can say I try to question it, but deep down I think the voices are accurate. I think they’re telling me where to look because there really is something there.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think it's possible for you to be mistaken about what the voices are telling you?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 12\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… I can say the word “possible,” because I know people can be mistaken. I’m not trying to say I’m some perfect person who can never misread something. But with this, it doesn’t feel like I’m mistaken, because it isn’t just the voices by themselves. It’s the wound-care charts. I’ve looked at them so many times, and there is a pattern there — the entries, the timing, the changes — and it’s the kind of thing management would not want someone like me noticing.

When my supervisor says, “Oh, that’s just late entries,” or “That’s just coding mistakes,” I hear that, but it doesn’t explain all of it. It feels like she’s trying to make it sound ordinary so I’ll stop looking. And I can tell they’re paying attention to what I write down now, what I flag, who I mention things to, because I’m the one who caught it.

So… could I be mistaken about what the voices are telling me? Maybe in some general way, like anybody could be mistaken about anything. But honestly, no, I don’t think I am. I think they’re pointing me toward what’s already in the records.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF23, F25.1, F31.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… it’s the voices, mostly. I know how that sounds, and I’ve been trying to explain it in my head in a way that doesn’t make me sound like I’m just being dramatic, but it’s been happening at work. I’m a nursing assistant, and I keep hearing voices coming through the call-light speaker, and sometimes through the hallway vents, like in the unit.\n\nThey’ll say things about patient records being changed — like which ones, or what was altered — and it’s not muffled or like a vague noise. It’s clear, like somebody is standing right next to me talking. I’ll look around because I think someone must be there, or maybe someone’s on the intercom, but the unit will be quiet, and nobody else reacts or hears anything. That’s what’s scaring me, because it feels so real in the moment, and then when I ask or check, it’s like… there’s no one there.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you hear ever tell you to do something or criticize you?\n\nPatient: It’s not really like they’re saying, “Go do this” or yelling insults at me, no. It’s more… they’re telling me things, like information I shouldn’t be hearing — which patient records have been changed, what was altered, that kind of thing. And it comes through the call-light speaker or sometimes the hallway vents, and it’s so clear that I’ll stop what I’m doing because it sounds like someone is right there beside me.\n\nBut then the unit is quiet, nobody else looks up, nobody reacts, and I’m standing there thinking, “How am I the only one hearing this?” So it doesn’t feel like criticism exactly, and not commands exactly. It feels like these voices are reporting something to me, and I can’t tell why I’m hearing it or where it’s actually coming from.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious, depressed, or irritable when you're not hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not depressed so much, at least not in the way people usually mean, but anxious and irritated, yes. Some days it’s like I wake up and I feel… switched on, like I’m finally awake and I can see what needs to be fixed at work. Like things are obvious to me and everyone else is moving too slowly or pretending not to notice.\n\nAnd then if my husband says, “Maybe you should rest,” or one of the nurses tells me to slow down or just do one thing at a time, I get really sharp with them. I snap before I even think about it, because in that moment it feels like they’re blocking something important, like they’re getting in the way of me handling what needs to be handled. Then later I can hear how I sounded, and I don’t like that, but at the time it feels urgent. It feels like I’m the only one taking it seriously.\n\nDoctor: Do you have periods where you feel extremely happy or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Extremely happy… I don’t know if I’d call it happy exactly. It’s more like I get this feeling that I’m finally awake, like my brain is turned on and I can see everything clearly, especially at work. Like I’m meant to fix things, or notice things other people are missing.\n\nOn those days I feel very driven, and it can feel good at first, like I have a purpose. But it turns irritable really fast. If my husband says I need to slow down, or if one of the nurses tells me to take a breath or focus on one thing, I snap at them. I hear myself getting sharp, like, “Why are you getting in my way?” because in that moment it feels like they’re blocking something important.\n\nSo… euphoric maybe isn’t the word I would use. It’s more like energized and sure of myself, like I’m supposed to handle everything, and then I get angry when people try to slow me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice a drop in your energy levels after these periods where you feel driven and like you can handle everything?\n\nPatient: Not right away, no. That’s the thing that scares my husband, I think, because after a full shift — and it’s not like those shifts are easy, I’m on my feet, answering call lights, moving patients, running around — I’ll come home and I still feel like there’s this motor in me.\n\nI’ll start cleaning the kitchen, then I’ll notice the counters need doing, then I’m rewriting notes or sorting through supplies I brought home or things I think need to be organized. And I’ll just keep going. I’ll pace around the kitchen while I’m talking, like I can’t just stand still, and my husband will be sitting there saying, “Please, just sit down for a minute,” or “You’ve already worked all day,” and I know he’s tired just watching me.\n\nBut in that moment I don’t feel tired. I feel like if I sit down, something important won’t get finished. It’s only later that I might realize I’ve been moving nonstop, but during it, no — it doesn’t feel like a drop. It feels like I have too much energy and nowhere for it to go.\n\nDoctor: Do these periods of feeling driven and energized come and go, or are they pretty much always there?\n\nPatient: They come and go, I think… but it’s been more like waves inside the same stretch of time, if that makes sense. This hasn’t been my whole life or anything. It’s been going on for about two months now.\n\nIt really seemed to start after I was asked to help lead the wound-care documentation rollout at work. Once that happened, it was like something clicked on in me. Some days it’s stronger — I feel like I have to keep pushing and fixing and organizing everything — and then other days it’s not as intense, but it still feels like it’s been hanging around since then. So not always there in the exact same way, but for these past couple months, it keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever believe things that might seem strange or unlikely to others, like conspiracy theories or thoughts that you're being followed or plotted against?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t call it a conspiracy theory exactly, because that makes it sound like I’m just making something up or sitting around imagining things. But yes, I do think there’s something going on with the wound-care charts.\n\nI’ve been looking at them so closely because of this rollout, and there’s a pattern there. I know there is. The way certain entries are late, or changed, or coded differently… it’s not random. It points to something management doesn’t want people to see. And when I’ve tried to bring it up, my supervisor keeps saying it’s just late documentation or coding mistakes, like I’m overthinking it.\n\nBut I can tell they’re watching what I write now. Not in a dramatic way like someone following me in a car or anything, but at work — the notes I make, the corrections I flag, who I talk to about it. It feels very clear to me that they know I caught something, and now they’re paying attention because they don’t want it coming out. So… I know how that might sound, but to me it doesn’t feel strange. It feels like I’m the only one actually seeing the pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the voices or the information they provide are specifically meant for you or about you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, that’s part of why I’m here, because it doesn’t feel like random noise or like I’m just overhearing something that has nothing to do with me. It feels directed at me.\n\nIt happens with the call-light speaker sometimes, or from the hallway vents. I know that sounds odd when I say it out loud, but it’s clear, like somebody standing right next to me and saying it where I can hear it. Not muffled like normal hallway noise. It’ll tell me which patient records have been changed, or which chart I need to look at again, and it feels like that information is being given to me specifically because I’m the one who’s supposed to catch it.\n\nAnd it’s not just when the unit is busy and there are people talking everywhere. Sometimes it’s quiet, or quiet enough that I know there isn’t someone there saying it, and nobody else reacts. Nobody turns their head, nobody answers, nobody says, “Did you hear that?” So then I’m standing there thinking, how am I the only one hearing this if it’s coming through so clearly? But it still feels meant for me, like it’s about what I’m finding in the records.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voices you're hearing could be your own mind trying to tell you something, rather than actual external voices?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to think of it that way, honestly. I’ve stood there and asked myself, “Is this just me? Is my brain trying to warn me because I’m stressed or because I’ve been looking at these charts too much?” I know that’s probably the reasonable explanation you’re trying to get at.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like my own thoughts. My thoughts feel like they’re inside my head, if that makes sense. This is different. It’ll be like someone standing right next to me telling me which patient record was changed or which chart I need to look at again.\n\nAnd the part that really scares me is it happens even when the unit is quiet enough that I can tell nobody is there saying it. I hear myself getting sharp, like, “Why are you getting in my way?” because in that moment it feels like they’re blocking something important.\n\nSo… euphoric maybe isn’t the word I would use. It’s more like energized and sure of myself, like I’m supposed to handle everything, and then I get angry when people try to slow me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice a drop in your energy levels after these periods where you feel driven and like you can handle everything?\n\nPatient: Not right away, no. That’s the thing that scares my husband, I think, because after a full shift — and it’s not like those shifts are easy, I’m on my feet, answering call lights, moving patients, running around — I’ll come home and I still feel like there’s this motor in me.\n\nI’ll start cleaning the kitchen, then I’ll notice the counters need doing, then I’m rewriting notes or sorting through supplies I brought home or things I think need to be organized. And I’ll just keep going. I’ll pace around the kitchen while I’m talking, like I can’t just stand still, and my husband will be sitting there saying, “Please, just sit down for a minute,” or “You’ve already worked all day,” and I know he’s tired just watching me.\n\nBut in that moment I don’t feel tired. I feel like if I sit down, something important won’t get finished. It’s only later that I might realize I’ve been moving nonstop, but during it, no — it doesn’t feel like a drop. It feels like I have too much energy and nowhere for it to go.\n\nDoctor: Do these periods of feeling driven and energized come and go, or are they pretty much always there?\n\nPatient: They come and go, I think… but it’s been more like waves inside the same stretch of time, if that makes sense. This hasn’t been my whole life or anything. It’s been going on for about two months now.\n\nIt really seemed to start after I was asked to help lead the wound-care documentation rollout at work. Once that happened, it was like something clicked on in me. Some days it’s stronger — I feel like I have to keep pushing and fixing and organizing everything — and then other days it’s not as intense, but it still feels like it’s been hanging around since then. So not always there in the exact same way, but for these past couple months, it keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever believe things that might seem strange or unlikely to others, like conspiracy theories or thoughts that you're being followed or plotted against?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t call it a conspiracy theory exactly, because that makes it sound like I’m just making something up or sitting around imagining things. But yes, I do think there’s something going on with the wound-care charts.\n\nI’ve been looking at them so closely because of this rollout, and there’s a pattern there. I know there is. The way certain entries are late, or changed, or coded differently… it’s not random. It points to something management doesn’t want people to see. And when I’ve tried to bring it up, my supervisor keeps saying it’s just late documentation or coding mistakes, like I’m overthinking it.\n\nBut I can tell they’re watching what I write now. Not in a dramatic way like someone following me in a car or anything, but at work — the notes I make, the corrections I flag, who I talk to about it. It feels very clear to me that they know I caught something, and now they’re paying attention because they don’t want it coming out. So… I know how that might sound, but to me it doesn’t feel strange. It feels like I’m the only one actually seeing the pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the voices or the information they provide are specifically meant for you or about you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, that’s part of why I’m here, because it doesn’t feel like random noise or like I’m just overhearing something that has nothing to do with me. It feels directed at me.\n\nIt happens with the call-light speaker sometimes, or from the hallway vents. I know that sounds odd when I say it out loud, but it’s clear, like somebody standing right next to me and saying it where I can hear it. Not muffled like normal hallway noise. It’ll tell me which patient records have been changed, or which chart I need to look at again, and it feels like that information is being given to me specifically because I’m the one who’s supposed to catch it.\n\nAnd it’s not just when the unit is busy and there are people talking everywhere. Sometimes it’s quiet, or quiet enough that I know there isn’t someone there saying it, and nobody else reacts. Nobody turns their head, nobody answers, nobody says, “Did you hear that?” So then I’m standing there thinking, how am I the only one hearing this if it’s coming through so clearly? But it still feels meant for me, like it’s about what I’m finding in the records.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voices you're hearing could be your own mind trying to tell you something, rather than actual external voices?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to think of it that way, honestly. I’ve stood there and asked myself, “Is this just me? Is my brain trying to warn me because I’m stressed or because I’ve been looking at these charts too much?” I know that’s probably the reasonable explanation you’re trying to get at.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like my own thoughts. My thoughts feel like they’re inside my head, if that makes sense. This is different. It sounds like it’s coming from outside me — like from the call-light speaker or from the hallway vents — and it’s clear, not like a vague feeling or an intuition. It’ll be like someone standing right next to me telling me which patient record was changed or which chart I need to look at again.\n\nAnd the part that really scares me is it happens even when the unit is quiet enough that I can tell nobody is there saying it. Nobody else reacts, nobody looks up, nobody says they heard anything. So I can understand why you’d ask if it could be my mind, but in the moment it feels external. It feels like an actual voice coming through to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the information the voices are giving you about patient records being changed might not be true?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know you’re asking if maybe the voices are wrong, or if maybe I’m putting too much weight on what they say. I can understand why you’d ask that. But the reason it’s hard for me to say, “Yes, it might not be true,” is because it matches what I’m already seeing in the wound-care charts.\n\nIt’s not like the information is coming out of nowhere. I’ve been looking at those charts over and over, and there are patterns — late entries, things changed, codes that don’t line up the way they should. And every time I try to bring it up, my supervisor just brushes it off like it’s normal documentation delay or somebody made a coding mistake. But it doesn’t feel like mistakes. It feels too organized, like there’s something there management doesn’t want anyone to notice.\n\nAnd since I’m the one who caught it, I can tell they’re paying attention to me now. What I write down, what I flag, who I talk to about it — it feels like they’re watching that because they know I found the pattern. So… could I say it’s possible the voices are giving me wrong information? I guess anything is “possible” in the way people say that. But honestly, no, I don’t really believe that. To me it feels like the voices are pointing me toward what’s already there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have any doubts about the accuracy of the information the voices are giving you?\n\nPatient: I have doubts for a second, maybe, because I know how it sounds when I say it out loud. I know other people don’t hear it, and I know my supervisor keeps saying it’s just late entries or coding mistakes, like it’s nothing. So yes, there’s a part of me that tries to stop and ask, “Am I getting this wrong?”\n\nBut then I go back to the wound-care charts, and the pattern is still there. It’s not just one little mistake. It’s the way things are entered, the timing, what gets changed, what doesn’t match. And the voices aren’t telling me something totally separate from that — they’re pointing me back to what I’m already seeing.\n\nSo I don’t really doubt the information in the way you probably mean. I know those charts are showing something management doesn’t want anyone to see. And when my supervisor brushes it off, that almost makes me feel more sure, because it feels like she’s trying to make it seem ordinary when it isn’t.\n\nAnd I can tell they’re watching me now — what I write down, what I flag, who I mention it to — because I’m the one who caught it. So… I can say I try to question it, but deep down I think the voices are accurate. I think they’re telling me where to look because there really is something there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible for you to be mistaken about what the voices are telling you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I can say the word “possible,” because I know people can be mistaken. I’m not trying to say I’m some perfect person who can never misread something. But with this, it doesn’t feel like I’m mistaken, because it isn’t just the voices by themselves. It’s the wound-care charts. I’ve looked at them so many times, and there is a pattern there — the entries, the timing, the changes — and it’s the kind of thing management would not want someone like me noticing.\n\nWhen my supervisor says, “Oh, that’s just late entries,” or “That’s just coding mistakes,” I hear that, but it doesn’t explain all of it. It feels like she’s trying to make it sound ordinary so I’ll stop looking. And I can tell they’re paying attention to what I write down now, what I flag, who I mention things to, because I’m the one who caught it.\n\nSo… could I be mistaken about what the voices are telling me? Maybe in some general way, like anybody could be mistaken about anything. But honestly, no, I don’t think I am. I think they’re pointing me toward what’s already in the records.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I mean… I’ve tried to think of other explanations because I know that’s what you’re asking me to do. Like, could it just be stress, or could I be reading too much into things, or could the voices be something my mind is making out of all the pressure at work? I can sit here and say those words, but they don’t really fit what’s happening.

Because the information isn’t random. It lines up with the wound-care charts. That’s the part I keep coming back to. If it was just voices saying strange things with no connection to anything, maybe I could dismiss it more easily. But I look at the charts and I see the same pattern again — the timing of entries, things being changed, the way certain codes don’t match. It’s there. I know it’s there.

And then when I bring it up, my supervisor acts like it’s nothing, like it’s just late documentation or ordinary coding mistakes. But that explanation feels too convenient. It doesn’t explain the whole pattern. It feels like she wants me to stop looking at it, like if she can make me feel silly or overdramatic, then I’ll drop it.

So… another explanation? I guess the explanation other people want me to accept is that it’s all just errors and delays and I’m connecting dots that don’t belong together. But I don’t believe that. I know those records are showing something management doesn’t want anyone to see. And I can tell they’re watching what I write and what I flag now, because I’m the one who caught it.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF23, F25.1, F31.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I know how that sounds, and I’ve been trying to explain it in my head in a way that doesn’t make me sound like I’m just being dramatic, but it’s been happening at work. I’m a nursing assistant, and I keep hearing voices coming through the call-light speaker, and sometimes through the hallway vents, like in the unit.\n\nThey’ll say things about patient records being changed — like which ones, or what was altered — and it’s not muffled or like a vague noise. It’s clear, like somebody is standing right next to me talking. I’ll look around because I think someone must be there, or maybe someone’s on the intercom, but the unit will be quiet, and nobody else reacts or hears anything. That’s what’s scaring me, because it feels so real in the moment, and then when I ask or check, it’s like… there’s no one there.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you hear ever tell you to do something or criticize you?\n\nPatient: It’s not really like they’re saying, “Go do this” or yelling insults at me, no. It’s more… they’re telling me things, like information I shouldn’t be hearing — which patient records have been changed, what was altered, that kind of thing. And it comes through the call-light speaker or sometimes the hallway vents, and it’s so clear that I’ll stop what I’m doing because it sounds like someone is right there beside me.\n\nBut then the unit is quiet, nobody else looks up, nobody reacts, and I’m standing there thinking, “How am I the only one hearing this?” So it doesn’t feel like criticism exactly, and not commands exactly. It feels like these voices are reporting something to me, and I can’t tell why I’m hearing it or where it’s actually coming from.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious, depressed, or irritable when you're not hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not depressed so much, at least not in the way people usually mean, but anxious and irritated, yes. Some days it’s like I wake up and I feel… switched on, like I’m finally awake and I can see what needs to be fixed at work. Like things are obvious to me and everyone else is moving too slowly or pretending not to notice.\n\nAnd then if my husband says, “Maybe you should rest,” or one of the nurses tells me to slow down or just do one thing at a time, I get really sharp with them. I snap before I even think about it, because in that moment it feels like they’re blocking something important, like they’re getting in the way of me handling what needs to be handled. Then later I can hear how I sounded, and I don’t like that, but at the time it feels urgent. It feels like I’m the only one taking it seriously.\n\nDoctor: Do you have periods where you feel extremely happy or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Extremely happy… I don’t know if I’d call it happy exactly. It’s more like I get this feeling that I’m finally awake, like my brain is turned on and I can see everything clearly, especially at work. Like I’m meant to fix things, or notice things other people are missing.\n\nOn those days I feel very driven, and it can feel good at first, like I have a purpose. But it turns irritable really fast. If my husband says I need to slow down, or if one of the nurses tells me to take a breath or focus on one thing, I snap at them. I hear myself getting sharp, like, “Why are you getting in my way?” because in that moment it feels like they’re blocking something important.\n\nSo… euphoric maybe isn’t the word I would use. It’s more like energized and sure of myself, like I’m supposed to handle everything, and then I get angry when people try to slow me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice a drop in your energy levels after these periods where you feel driven and like you can handle everything?\n\nPatient: Not right away, no. That’s the thing that scares my husband, I think, because after a full shift — and it’s not like those shifts are easy, I’m on my feet, answering call lights, moving patients, running around — I’ll come home and I still feel like there’s this motor in me.\n\nI’ll start cleaning the kitchen, then I’ll notice the counters need doing, then I’m rewriting notes or sorting through supplies I brought home or things I think need to be organized. And I’ll just keep going. I’ll pace around the kitchen while I’m talking, like I can’t just stand still, and my husband will be sitting there saying, “Please, just sit down for a minute,” or “You’ve already worked all day,” and I know he’s tired just watching me.\n\nBut in that moment I don’t feel tired. I feel like if I sit down, something important won’t get finished. It’s only later that I might realize I’ve been moving nonstop, but during it, no — it doesn’t feel like a drop. It feels like I have too much energy and nowhere for it to go.\n\nDoctor: Do these periods of feeling driven and energized come and go, or are they pretty much always there?\n\nPatient: They come and go, I think… but it’s been more like waves inside the same stretch of time, if that makes sense. This hasn’t been my whole life or anything. It’s been going on for about two months now.\n\nIt really seemed to start after I was asked to help lead the wound-care documentation rollout at work. Once that happened, it was like something clicked on in me. Some days it’s stronger — I feel like I have to keep pushing and fixing and organizing everything — and then other days it’s not as intense, but it still feels like it’s been hanging around since then. So not always there in the exact same way, but for these past couple months, it keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever believe things that might seem strange or unlikely to others, like conspiracy theories or thoughts that you're being followed or plotted against?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t call it a conspiracy theory exactly, because that makes it sound like I’m just making something up or sitting around imagining things. But yes, I do think there’s something going on with the wound-care charts.\n\nI’ve been looking at them so closely because of this rollout, and there’s a pattern there. I know there is. The way certain entries are late, or changed, or coded differently… it’s not random. It points to something management doesn’t want people to see. And when I’ve tried to bring it up, my supervisor keeps saying it’s just late documentation or coding mistakes, like I’m overthinking it.\n\nBut I can tell they’re watching what I write now. Not in a dramatic way like someone following me in a car or anything, but at work — the notes I make, the corrections I flag, who I talk to about it. It feels very clear to me that they know I caught something, and now they’re paying attention because they don’t want it coming out. So… I know how that might sound, but to me it doesn’t feel strange. It feels like I’m the only one actually seeing the pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the voices or the information they provide are specifically meant for you or about you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, that’s part of why I’m here, because it doesn’t feel like random noise or like I’m just overhearing something that has nothing to do with me. It feels directed at me.\n\nIt happens with the call-light speaker sometimes, or from the hallway vents. I know that sounds odd when I say it out loud, but it’s clear, like somebody standing right next to me and saying it where I can hear it. Not muffled like normal hallway noise. It’ll tell me which patient records have been changed, or which chart I need to look at again, and it feels like that information is being given to me specifically because I’m the one who’s supposed to catch it.\n\nAnd it’s not just when the unit is busy and there are people talking everywhere. Sometimes it’s quiet, or quiet enough that I know there isn’t someone there saying it, and nobody else reacts. Nobody turns their head, nobody answers, nobody says, “Did you hear that?” So then I’m standing there thinking, how am I the only one hearing this if it’s coming through so clearly? But it still feels meant for me, like it’s about what I’m finding in the records.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voices you're hearing could be your own mind trying to tell you something, rather than actual external voices?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to think of it that way, honestly. I’ve stood there and asked myself, “Is this just me? Is my brain trying to warn me because I’m stressed or because I’ve been looking at these charts too much?” I know that’s probably the reasonable explanation you’re trying to get at.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like my own thoughts. My thoughts feel like they’re inside my head, if that makes sense. This is different. It sounds like it’s coming from outside me — like from the call-light speaker or from the hallway vents — and it’s clear, not like a vague feeling or an intuition. It’ll be like someone standing right next to me telling me which patient record was changed or which chart I need to look at again.\n\nAnd the part that really scares me is it happens even when the unit is quiet enough that I can tell nobody is there saying it. Nobody else reacts, nobody looks up, nobody says they heard anything. So I can understand why you’d ask if it could be my mind, but in the moment it feels external. It feels like an actual voice coming through to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the information the voices are giving you about patient records being changed might not be true?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know you’re asking if maybe the voices are wrong, or if maybe I’m putting too much weight on what they say. I can understand why you’d ask that. But the reason it’s hard for me to say, “Yes, it might not be true,” is because it matches what I’m already seeing in the wound-care charts.\n\nIt’s not like the information is coming out of nowhere. I’ve been looking at those charts over and over, and there are patterns — late entries, things changed, codes that don’t line up the way they should. And every time I try to bring it up, my supervisor just brushes it off like it’s normal documentation delay or somebody made a coding mistake. But it doesn’t feel like mistakes. It feels too organized, like there’s something there management doesn’t want anyone to notice.\n\nAnd since I’m the one who caught it, I can tell they’re paying attention to me now. What I write down, what I flag, who I talk to about it — it feels like they’re watching that because they know I found the pattern. So… could I say it’s possible the voices are giving me wrong information? I guess anything is “possible” in the way people say that. But honestly, no, I don’t really believe that. To me it feels like the voices are pointing me toward what’s already there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have any doubts about the accuracy of the information the voices are giving you?\n\nPatient: I have doubts for a second, maybe, because I know how it sounds when I say it out loud. I know other people don’t hear it, and I know my supervisor keeps saying it’s just late entries or coding mistakes, like it’s nothing. So yes, there’s a part of me that tries to stop and ask, “Am I getting this wrong?”\n\nBut then I go back to the wound-care charts, and the pattern is still there. It’s not just one little mistake. It’s the way things are entered, the timing, what gets changed, what doesn’t match. And the voices aren’t telling me something totally separate from that — they’re pointing me back to what I’m already seeing.\n\nSo I don’t really doubt the information in the way you probably mean. I know those charts are showing something management doesn’t want anyone to see. And when my supervisor brushes it off, that almost makes me feel more sure, because it feels like she’s trying to make it seem ordinary when it isn’t.\n\nAnd I can tell they’re watching me now — what I write down, what I flag, who I mention it to — because I’m the one who caught it. So… I can say I try to question it, but deep down I think the voices are accurate. I think they’re telling me where to look because there really is something there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible for you to be mistaken about what the voices are telling you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I can say the word “possible,” because I know people can be mistaken. I’m not trying to say I’m some perfect person who can never misread something. But with this, it doesn’t feel like I’m mistaken, because it isn’t just the voices by themselves. It’s the wound-care charts. I’ve looked at them so many times, and there is a pattern there — the entries, the timing, the changes — and it’s the kind of thing management would not want someone like me noticing.\n\nWhen my supervisor says, “Oh, that’s just late entries,” or “That’s just coding mistakes,” I hear that, but it doesn’t explain all of it. It feels like she’s trying to make it sound ordinary so I’ll stop looking. And I can tell they’re paying attention to what I write down now, what I flag, who I mention things to, because I’m the one who caught it.\n\nSo… could I be mistaken about what the voices are telling me? Maybe in some general way, like anybody could be mistaken about anything. But honestly, no, I don’t think I am. I think they’re pointing me toward what’s already in the records.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any other explanation for the voices and the information they're giving you, other than what you currently believe?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’ve tried to think of other explanations because I know that’s what you’re asking me to do. Like, could it just be stress, or could I be reading too much into things, or could the voices be something my mind is making out of all the pressure at work? I can sit here and say those words, but they don’t really fit what’s happening.\n\nBecause the information isn’t random. It lines up with the wound-care charts. That’s the part I keep coming back to. If it was just voices saying strange things with no connection to anything, maybe I could dismiss it more easily. But I look at the charts and I see the same pattern again — the timing of entries, things being changed, the way certain codes don’t match. It’s there. I know it’s there.\n\nAnd then when I bring it up, my supervisor acts like it’s nothing, like it’s just late documentation or ordinary coding mistakes. But that explanation feels too convenient. It doesn’t explain the whole pattern. It feels like she wants me to stop looking at it, like if she can make me feel silly or overdramatic, then I’ll drop it.\n\nSo… another explanation? I guess the explanation other people want me to accept is that it’s all just errors and delays and I’m connecting dots that don’t belong together. But I don’t believe that. I know those records are showing something management doesn’t want anyone to see. And I can tell they’re watching what I write and what I flag now, because I’m the one who caught it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F23", "F25.1", "F31.2"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I can consider it in the sense that I hear what you’re saying — like, yes, on paper, late entries happen, coding mistakes happen, people chart after the fact, things get corrected. I know that. I’ve worked around charts long enough to know not every discrepancy means something sinister.

But with these wound-care charts, it doesn’t feel straightforward. That’s the part I keep trying to explain. If it was just one late note, or one wrong code, or one nurse forgetting to enter something until the end of the shift, I wouldn’t be sitting here making such a big deal out of it. It’s the pattern. It’s the timing of what gets changed, what doesn’t match, the way the explanations always come after I ask about it.

And when my supervisor says, “It’s just late entries,” or “It’s just coding errors,” I try to take that in, but it feels like she’s smoothing it over too quickly. Like she wants me to accept the simple explanation and stop looking. And now I feel like they’re paying attention to what I write down and what I flag because I’m the one who noticed it.

So… could there be a simpler explanation? Maybe for one or two things, yes. But for all of it together? No. I really think the charts are showing something management doesn’t want anyone to see.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… it’s the voices, mostly. I know how that sounds, and I’ve been trying to explain it in my head in a way that doesn’t make me sound like I’m just being dramatic, but it’s been happening at work. I’m a nursing assistant, and I keep hearing voices coming through the call-light speaker, and sometimes through the hallway vents, like in the unit.\n\nThey’ll say things about patient records being changed — like which ones, or what was altered — and it’s not muffled or like a vague noise. It’s clear, like somebody is standing right next to me talking. I’ll look around because I think someone must be there, or maybe someone’s on the intercom, but the unit will be quiet, and nobody else reacts or hears anything. That’s what’s scaring me, because it feels so real in the moment, and then when I ask or check, it’s like… there’s no one there.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you hear ever tell you to do something or criticize you?\n\nPatient: It’s not really like they’re saying, “Go do this” or yelling insults at me, no. It’s more… they’re telling me things, like information I shouldn’t be hearing — which patient records have been changed, what was altered, that kind of thing. And it comes through the call-light speaker or sometimes the hallway vents, and it’s so clear that I’ll stop what I’m doing because it sounds like someone is right there beside me.\n\nBut then the unit is quiet, nobody else looks up, nobody reacts, and I’m standing there thinking, “How am I the only one hearing this?” So it doesn’t feel like criticism exactly, and not commands exactly. It feels like these voices are reporting something to me, and I can’t tell why I’m hearing it or where it’s actually coming from.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious, depressed, or irritable when you're not hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not depressed so much, at least not in the way people usually mean, but anxious and irritated, yes. Some days it’s like I wake up and I feel… switched on, like I’m finally awake and I can see what needs to be fixed at work. Like things are obvious to me and everyone else is moving too slowly or pretending not to notice.\n\nAnd then if my husband says, “Maybe you should rest,” or one of the nurses tells me to slow down or just do one thing at a time, I get really sharp with them. I snap before I even think about it, because in that moment it feels like they’re blocking something important, like they’re getting in the way of me handling what needs to be handled. Then later I can hear how I sounded, and I don’t like that, but at the time it feels urgent. It feels like I’m the only one taking it seriously.\n\nDoctor: Do you have periods where you feel extremely happy or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Extremely happy… I don’t know if I’d call it happy exactly. It’s more like I get this feeling that I’m finally awake, like my brain is turned on and I can see everything clearly, especially at work. Like I’m meant to fix things, or notice things other people are missing.\n\nOn those days I feel very driven, and it can feel good at first, like I have a purpose. But it turns irritable really fast. If my husband says I need to slow down, or if one of the nurses tells me to take a breath or focus on one thing, I snap at them. I hear myself getting sharp, like, “Why are you getting in my way?” because in that moment it feels like they’re blocking something important.\n\nSo… euphoric maybe isn’t the word I would use. It’s more like energized and sure of myself, like I’m supposed to handle everything, and then I get angry when people try to slow me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice a drop in your energy levels after these periods where you feel driven and like you can handle everything?\n\nPatient: Not right away, no. That’s the thing that scares my husband, I think, because after a full shift — and it’s not like those shifts are easy, I’m on my feet, answering call lights, moving patients, running around — I’ll come home and I still feel like there’s this motor in me.\n\nI’ll start cleaning the kitchen, then I’ll notice the counters need doing, then I’m rewriting notes or sorting through supplies I brought home or things I think need to be organized. And I’ll just keep going. I’ll pace around the kitchen while I’m talking, like I can’t just stand still, and my husband will be sitting there saying, “Please, just sit down for a minute,” or “You’ve already worked all day,” and I know he’s tired just watching me.\n\nBut in that moment I don’t feel tired. I feel like if I sit down, something important won’t get finished. It’s only later that I might realize I’ve been moving nonstop, but during it, no — it doesn’t feel like a drop. It feels like I have too much energy and nowhere for it to go.\n\nDoctor: Do these periods of feeling driven and energized come and go, or are they pretty much always there?\n\nPatient: They come and go, I think… but it’s been more like waves inside the same stretch of time, if that makes sense. This hasn’t been my whole life or anything. It’s been going on for about two months now.\n\nIt really seemed to start after I was asked to help lead the wound-care documentation rollout at work. Once that happened, it was like something clicked on in me. Some days it’s stronger — I feel like I have to keep pushing and fixing and organizing everything — and then other days it’s not as intense, but it still feels like it’s been hanging around since then. So not always there in the exact same way, but for these past couple months, it keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever believe things that might seem strange or unlikely to others, like conspiracy theories or thoughts that you're being followed or plotted against?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t call it a conspiracy theory exactly, because that makes it sound like I’m just making something up or sitting around imagining things. But yes, I do think there’s something going on with the wound-care charts.\n\nI’ve been looking at them so closely because of this rollout, and there’s a pattern there. I know there is. The way certain entries are late, or changed, or coded differently… it’s not random. It points to something management doesn’t want people to see. And when I’ve tried to bring it up, my supervisor keeps saying it’s just late documentation or coding mistakes, like I’m overthinking it.\n\nBut I can tell they’re watching what I write now. Not in a dramatic way like someone following me in a car or anything, but at work — the notes I make, the corrections I flag, who I talk to about it. It feels very clear to me that they know I caught something, and now they’re paying attention because they don’t want it coming out. So… I know how that might sound, but to me it doesn’t feel strange. It feels like I’m the only one actually seeing the pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the voices or the information they provide are specifically meant for you or about you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, that’s part of why I’m here, because it doesn’t feel like random noise or like I’m just overhearing something that has nothing to do with me. It feels directed at me.\n\nIt happens with the call-light speaker sometimes, or from the hallway vents. I know that sounds odd when I say it out loud, but it’s clear, like somebody standing right next to me and saying it where I can hear it. Not muffled like normal hallway noise. It’ll tell me which patient records have been changed, or which chart I need to look at again, and it feels like that information is being given to me specifically because I’m the one who’s supposed to catch it.\n\nAnd it’s not just when the unit is busy and there are people talking everywhere. Sometimes it’s quiet, or quiet enough that I know there isn’t someone there saying it, and nobody else reacts. Nobody turns their head, nobody answers, nobody says, “Did you hear that?” So then I’m standing there thinking, how am I the only one hearing this if it’s coming through so clearly? But it still feels meant for me, like it’s about what I’m finding in the records.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voices you're hearing could be your own mind trying to tell you something, rather than actual external voices?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to think of it that way, honestly. I’ve stood there and asked myself, “Is this just me? Is my brain trying to warn me because I’m stressed or because I’ve been looking at these charts too much?” I know that’s probably the reasonable explanation you’re trying to get at.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like my own thoughts. My thoughts feel like they’re inside my head, if that makes sense. This is different. It sounds like it’s coming from outside me — like from the call-light speaker or from the hallway vents — and it’s clear, not like a vague feeling or an intuition. It’ll be like someone standing right next to me telling me which patient record was changed or which chart I need to look at again.\n\nAnd the part that really scares me is it happens even when the unit is quiet enough that I can tell nobody is there saying it. Nobody else reacts, nobody looks up, nobody says they heard anything. So I can understand why you’d ask if it could be my mind, but in the moment it feels external. It feels like an actual voice coming through to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the information the voices are giving you about patient records being changed might not be true?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know you’re asking if maybe the voices are wrong, or if maybe I’m putting too much weight on what they say. I can understand why you’d ask that. But the reason it’s hard for me to say, “Yes, it might not be true,” is because it matches what I’m already seeing in the wound-care charts.\n\nIt’s not like the information is coming out of nowhere. I’ve been looking at those charts over and over, and there are patterns — late entries, things changed, codes that don’t line up the way they should. To me it feels like the voices are pointing me toward what’s already there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have any doubts about the accuracy of the information the voices are giving you?\n\nPatient: I have doubts for a second, maybe, because I know how it sounds when I say it out loud. I know other people don’t hear it, and I know my supervisor keeps saying it’s just late entries or coding mistakes, like it’s nothing. So yes, there’s a part of me that tries to stop and ask, “Am I getting this wrong?”\n\nBut then I go back to the wound-care charts, and the pattern is still there. It’s not just one little mistake. It’s the way things are entered, the timing, what gets changed, what doesn’t match. And the voices aren’t telling me something totally separate from that — they’re pointing me back to what I’m already seeing.\n\nSo I don’t really doubt the information in the way you probably mean. I know those charts are showing something management doesn’t want anyone to see. And when my supervisor brushes it off, that almost makes me feel more sure, because it feels like she’s trying to make it seem ordinary when it isn’t.\n\nAnd I can tell they’re watching me now — what I write down, what I flag, who I mention it to — because I’m the one who caught it. So… I can say I try to question it, but deep down I think the voices are accurate. I think they’re telling me where to look because there really is something there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible for you to be mistaken about what the voices are telling you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I can say the word “possible,” because I know people can be mistaken. I’m not trying to say I’m some perfect person who can never misread something. But with this, it doesn’t feel like I’m mistaken, because it isn’t just the voices by themselves. It’s the wound-care charts. I’ve looked at them so many times, and there is a pattern there — the entries, the timing, the changes — and it’s the kind of thing management would not want someone like me noticing.\n\nWhen my supervisor says, “Oh, that’s just late entries,” or “That’s just coding mistakes,” I hear that, but it doesn’t explain all of it. It feels like she’s trying to make it sound ordinary so I’ll stop looking. And I can tell they’re paying attention to what I write down now, what I flag, who I mention things to, because I’m the one who caught it.\n\nSo… could I be mistaken about what the voices are telling me? Maybe in some general way, like anybody could be mistaken about anything. But honestly, no, I don’t think I am. I think they’re pointing me toward what’s already in the records.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any other explanation for the voices and the information they're giving you, other than what you currently believe?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’ve tried to think of other explanations because I know that’s what you’re asking me to do. Like, could it just be stress, or could I be reading too much into things, or could the voices be something my mind is making out of all the pressure at work? I can sit here and say those words, but they don’t really fit what’s happening.\n\nBecause the information isn’t random. It lines up with the wound-care charts. That’s the part I keep coming back to. If it was just voices saying strange things with no connection to anything, maybe I could dismiss it more easily. But I look at the charts and I see the same pattern again — the timing of entries, things being changed, the way certain codes don’t match. It’s there. I know it’s there.\n\nAnd then when I bring it up, my supervisor acts like it’s nothing, like it’s just late documentation or ordinary coding mistakes. But that explanation feels too convenient. It doesn’t explain the whole pattern. It feels like she wants me to stop looking at it, like if she can make me feel silly or overdramatic, then I’ll drop it.\n\nSo… another explanation? I guess the explanation other people want me to accept is that it’s all just errors and delays and I’m connecting dots that don’t belong together. But I don’t believe that. I know those records are showing something management doesn’t want anyone to see. And I can tell they’re watching what I write and what I flag now, because I’m the one who caught it.\n\nDoctor: Can you consider the possibility that there might be a more straightforward explanation for the discrepancies in the wound-care charts that doesn't involve a deliberate attempt to hide something?\n\nPatient: I can consider it in the sense that I hear what you’re saying — like, yes, on paper, late entries happen, coding mistakes happen, people chart after the fact, things get corrected. I know that. I’ve worked around charts long enough to know not every discrepancy means something sinister.\n\nBut with these wound-care charts, it doesn’t feel straightforward. That’s the part I keep trying to explain. If it was just one late note, or one wrong code, or one nurse forgetting to enter something until the end of the shift, I wouldn’t be sitting here making such a big deal out of it. It’s the pattern. It’s the timing of what gets changed, what doesn’t match, the way the explanations always come after I ask about it.\n\nAnd when my supervisor says, “It’s just late entries,” or “It’s just coding errors,” I try to take that in, but it feels like she’s smoothing it over too quickly. Like she wants me to accept the simple explanation and stop looking. And now I feel like they’re paying attention to what I write down and what I flag because I’m the one who noticed it.\n\nSo… could there be a simpler explanation? Maybe for one or two things, yes. But for all of it together? No. I really think the charts are showing something management doesn’t want anyone to see.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… that’s hard, because part of me wants to say yes, of course, if someone could sit down with me and go line by line through the wound-care charts and show me why each thing happened, maybe I would feel differently. Not just wave their hand and say, “late entry,” but actually show me — this person documented at this time because of this, this code changed because of that, this discrepancy has a clear reason. If it all truly matched up and there wasn’t anything being hidden, then I guess I would have to look at that.

But the problem is, that’s not what’s been happening. What happens is I notice something, I flag it, and then suddenly the explanation is too quick, too neat. My supervisor says it’s just coding mistakes or delayed charting, but it doesn’t explain the whole pattern. It’s the timing, the changes, the way certain things show up after the fact. I keep coming back to that because it’s not one little mistake. It’s a pattern.

And honestly, even if they brought someone in to “explain” it, I’d worry about whether that person was just trying to smooth it over too. Because now I know they’re watching what I write down and what I flag. I can tell. I’m the one who caught it, so of course they’re paying attention to me now.

So… what would change my mind? Maybe complete transparency, like someone independent going through the charts with me and showing me that every single piece has an ordinary explanation. But I don’t know if they would ever really allow that, because I think the charts are showing something management doesn’t want anyone to see.
