Some days it’s stronger — I feel like I have to keep pushing and fixing and organizing everything — and then other days it’s not as intense, but it still feels like it’s been hanging around since then. So not always there in the exact same way, but for these past couple months, it keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever believe things that might seem strange or unlikely to others, like conspiracy theories or thoughts that you're being followed or plotted against?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t call it a conspiracy theory exactly, because that makes it sound like I’m just making something up or sitting around imagining things. But yes, I do think there’s something going on with the wound-care charts.\n\nI’ve been looking at them so closely because of this rollout, and there’s a pattern there. I know there is. The way certain entries are late, or changed, or coded differently… it’s not random. It points to something management doesn’t want people to see. And when I’ve tried to bring it up, my supervisor keeps saying it’s just late documentation or coding mistakes, like I’m overthinking it.\n\nBut I can tell they’re watching what I write now. Not in a dramatic way like someone following me in a car or anything, but at work — the notes I make, the corrections I flag, who I talk to about it. It feels very clear to me that they know I caught something, and now they’re paying attention because they don’t want it coming out. So… I know how that might sound, but to me it doesn’t feel strange. It feels like I’m the only one actually seeing the pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the voices or the information they provide are specifically meant for you or about you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, that’s part of why I’m here, because it doesn’t feel like random noise or like I’m just overhearing something that has nothing to do with me. Not just wave their hand and say, “late entry,” but actually show me — this person documented at this time because of this, this code changed because of that, this discrepancy has a clear reason. What happens is I notice something, I flag it, and then suddenly the explanation is too quick, too neat. I’m not saying I’d just refuse to hear it. But the charts aren’t just showing random mistakes. It’s the timing, the way the entries get changed, the way certain things don’t match, and then suddenly there’s this neat explanation after I’ve already flagged it. That doesn’t feel innocent to me.\n\nSo if someone could really sit with me and explain every discrepancy in a way that made sense, maybe I’d have to think about it. I’m the one who caught it, and once you catch something like that, people don’t just ignore you. That’s the part I can’t just set aside because someone tells me it’s all routine paperwork problems.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely energetic or irritable and didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. Not every day, but there are days where it feels like I’m finally awake, like my mind clicks on and I can see what needs to be fixed, especially at work. It’s not just regular energy, it’s more like this pressure inside me that says, “Get up, do it now, don’t let people miss this.”\n\nAnd on those days I don’t feel like I need much sleep. I’m not trying to be unreasonable. If someone came in from the outside and went through the wound-care charts carefully and could show, not just say, but show that there was no wrongdoing, I would sit there and look at it.\n\nBut I guess the hard part is that “independent” doesn’t always feel independent to me. I’ll be tired in a normal sense, maybe, but it doesn’t matter because I feel like there’s something important I’m supposed to be doing. Like if I slow down, something is going to get overlooked.\n\nBut then if my husband tells me I need to rest, or if a nurse at work tells me to slow down or leave something alone, I get really irritated. I snap. I hear myself doing it sometimes, but in the moment it feels like they’re blocking me from doing something important, like they don’t understand the urgency. It’s not just being annoyed — it feels like they’re getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about the wound-care charts would change if an independent auditor found no evidence of wrongdoing?\n\nPatient: I mean… if it was truly independent, I would have to hear what they said. I’m not trying to be unreasonable. If someone came in from the outside and went through the wound-care charts carefully and could show, not just say, but show that there was no wrongdoing, I would sit there and look at it.\n\nBut I guess the hard part is that “independent” doesn’t always feel independent to me. People say that word like it automatically means nobody has a reason to protect management, but I’ve seen how things get smoothed over. I know what I saw in those charts. It’s the pattern — the timing of the changes, the late entries, the coding explanations that only come after I point something out. My supervisor keeps saying it’s just normal documentation problems, but it doesn’t feel normal when it keeps lining up like that.\n\nAnd honestly, once I caught it, I could tell they started watching what I write and what I flag. That changes everything, because then any audit could also be about containing it, making me look like I’m reading too much into it, instead of actually looking at what the charts are showing.\n\nSo… would it change my mind? Maybe a little, if I really trusted the person and they explained every piece clearly. But if they just came back with “no evidence of wrongdoing” and expected me to drop it, no. Because I know those charts are showing something management doesn’t want people to see. That’s not something I can just ignore because someone puts a clean report on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your interpretation of the patterns in the wound-care charts is incorrect, even if the voices are telling you something?\n\nPatient: I mean… I understand why you’re asking that, and I can say the words that, yes, technically anybody can misread something. I’m not saying I’m incapable of ever being wrong about a chart or a code or one late entry. I know paperwork gets messy. I’ve worked around enough nurses and documentation to know things don’t always line up perfectly.\n\nBut this doesn’t feel like just me interpreting one odd thing the wrong way. That’s the part I keep trying to explain. It’s the pattern. It’s how the wound-care charts change, and then when I bring it up, there’s suddenly an explanation ready — late entries, coding mistakes, normal corrections — but it doesn’t explain why it keeps happening in a way that points in the same direction. It’s like they want me to accept each piece separately, like, “Oh, this one is nothing, that one is nothing,” but when you put them together, it shows something management doesn’t want people to see.\n\nAnd even with the voices… I know you’re asking if maybe that could be affecting how I see it. I can understand that question. But I don’t feel like the charts only matter because of that. I saw the discrepancies. I wrote down what didn’t match. And after I started catching it, I could tell they were watching what I wrote and what I flagged. That’s not just a feeling floating in the air — it lines up with what happened after I noticed the pattern.\n\nSo… possible? I guess in some abstract way, yes. But honestly, no, I don’t think I’m wrong about this. I think those charts are showing something, and I think management knows it, and that’s why they keep trying to make it sound like routine documentation errors.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think there's any chance that your beliefs about the wound-care charts are not based in reality?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I… I don’t really think so, no. I know how that sounds, because you’re asking if there’s some chance I’ve built this up in my head or connected things that aren’t actually connected. I understand the question. But when I look at those wound-care charts, it isn’t like I’m inventing something out of nothing.

And after I caught it, after I started writing things down and flagging what didn’t match, I could tell they were watching what I wrote. Not openly, not like someone saying, “We’re watching you,” but in the way people act when they know you saw something. The way my notes get questioned, the way they suddenly pay attention to what I’m documenting. It feels very clear to me that management doesn’t want anyone to see what those charts are showing.

So no… I don’t think it’s not based in reality. I think the reality is there, in the charts, and people keep trying to explain it away.

That’s the thing that scares my husband, I think, because after a full shift — and it’s not like those shifts are easy, I’m on my feet, answering call lights, moving patients, running around — I’ll come home and I still feel like there’s this motor in me.\n\nI’ll start cleaning the kitchen, then I’ll notice the counters need doing, then I’m rewriting notes or sorting through supplies I brought home or things I think need to be organized. Some days it’s stronger — I feel like I have to keep pushing and fixing and organizing everything — and then other days it’s not as intense, but it still feels like it’s been hanging around since then. So not always there in the exact same way, but for these past couple months, it keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever believe things that might seem strange or unlikely to others, like conspiracy theories or thoughts that you're being followed or plotted against?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t call it a conspiracy theory exactly, because that makes it sound like I’m just making something up or sitting around imagining things. Not in a dramatic way like someone following me in a car or anything, but at work — the notes I make, the corrections I flag, who I talk to about it. It feels very clear to me that they know I caught something, and now they’re paying attention because they don’t want it coming out. So… I know how that might sound, but to me it doesn’t feel strange. I mean… yes, that’s part of why I’m here, because it doesn’t feel like random noise or like I’m just overhearing something that has nothing to do with me. Sometimes it’s quiet, or quiet enough that I know there isn’t someone there saying it, and nobody else reacts. But it still feels meant for me, like it’s about what I’m finding in the records.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voices you're hearing could be your own mind trying to tell you something, rather than actual external voices?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to think of it that way, honestly. So… I can say I try to question it, but deep down I think the voices are accurate. I think they’re telling me where to look because there really is something there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible for you to be mistaken about what the voices are telling you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I can say the word “possible,” because I know people can be mistaken. I’m not trying to say I’m some perfect person who can never misread something. But with this, it doesn’t feel like I’m mistaken, because it isn’t just the voices by themselves. It’s the wound-care charts. I’ve looked at them so many times, and there is a pattern there — the entries, the timing, the changes — and it’s the kind of thing management would not want someone like me noticing.\n\nWhen my supervisor says, “Oh, that’s just late entries,” or “That’s just coding mistakes,” I hear that, but it doesn’t explain all of it. It feels like she’s trying to make it sound ordinary so I’ll stop looking. And I can tell they’re paying attention to what I write down now, what I flag, who I mention things to, because I’m the one who caught it.\n\nSo… could I be mistaken about what the voices are telling me? Maybe in some general way, like anybody could be mistaken about anything. But honestly, no, I don’t think I am. I guess the explanation other people want me to accept is that it’s all just errors and delays and I’m connecting dots that don’t belong together. But I don’t believe that. I know those records are showing something management doesn’t want anyone to see. That’s the part I keep trying to explain. If it was just one late note, or one wrong code, or one nurse forgetting to enter something until the end of the shift, I wouldn’t be sitting here making such a big deal out of it. It’s the pattern. It’s the timing of what gets changed, what doesn’t match, the way the explanations always come after I ask about it.\n\nAnd when my supervisor says, “It’s just late entries,” or “It’s just coding errors,” I try to take that in, but it feels like she’s smoothing it over too quickly. Like she wants me to accept the simple explanation and stop looking. And now I feel like they’re paying attention to what I write down and what I flag because I’m the one who noticed it.\n\nSo… could there be a simpler explanation? Maybe for one or two things, yes. But for all of it together? No. I really think the charts are showing something management doesn’t want anyone to see.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any situation or evidence that would make you question or change your beliefs about what's going on with the wound-care charts?\n\nPatient: I mean… that’s hard, because part of me wants to say yes, of course, if someone could sit down with me and go line by line through the wound-care charts and show me why each thing happened, maybe I would feel differently. Not just wave their hand and say, “late entry,” but actually show me — this person documented at this time because of this, this code changed because of that, this discrepancy has a clear reason. If it all truly matched up and there wasn’t anything being hidden, then I guess I would have to look at that.\n\nBut the problem is, that’s not what’s been happening. What happens is I notice something, I flag it, and then suddenly the explanation is too quick, too neat. My supervisor says it’s just coding mistakes or delayed charting, but it doesn’t explain the whole pattern. It’s the timing, the changes, the way certain things show up after the fact. I keep coming back to that because it’s not one little mistake. It’s a pattern.\n\nAnd honestly, even if they brought someone in to “explain” it, I’d worry about whether that person was just trying to smooth it over too. Because now I know they’re watching what I write down and what I flag. I can tell. I’m the one who caught it, so of course they’re paying attention to me now.\n\nSo… what would change my mind? Maybe complete transparency, like someone independent going through the charts with me and showing me that every single piece has an ordinary explanation. But I don’t know if they would ever really allow that, because I think the charts are showing something management doesn’t want anyone to see.\n\nDoctor: Would you change your mind about the wound-care charts if someone could provide a clear and reasonable explanation for the discrepancies you've found?\n\nPatient: If someone gave me a clear explanation, I would try to listen. But I’d still wonder who was giving the explanation and why. Because I can tell they’re watching what I write now. I’m the one who caught it, and once you catch something like that, people don’t just ignore you. They start paying attention to what you document and what you flag.\n\nSo yes, maybe in theory, but honestly… I know those wound-care charts are showing a pattern management doesn’t want anyone to see. That’s the part I can’t just set aside because someone tells me it’s all routine paperwork problems.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely energetic or irritable and didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. Not every day, but there are days where it feels like I’m finally awake, like my mind clicks on and I can see what needs to be fixed, especially at work. It’s not just regular energy, it’s more like this pressure inside me that says, “Get up, do it now, don’t let people miss this.”\n\nAnd on those days I don’t feel like I need much sleep. It’s not just being annoyed — it feels like they’re getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about the wound-care charts would change if an independent auditor found no evidence of wrongdoing?\n\nPatient: I mean… if it was truly independent, I would have to hear what they said. I’m not trying to be unreasonable. I’m the one who caught it, and once you catch something like that, people don’t just ignore you. They start paying attention to what you document and what you flag.\n\nSo yes, maybe in theory, but honestly… I know those wound-care charts are showing a pattern management doesn’t want anyone to see. That’s the part I can’t just set aside because someone tells me it’s all routine paperwork problems.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely energetic or irritable and didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. Not every day, but there are days where it feels like I’m finally awake, like my mind clicks on and I can see what needs to be fixed, especially at work. It’s not just regular energy, it’s more like this pressure inside me that says, “Get up, do it now, don’t let people miss this.”\n\nAnd on those days I don’t feel like I need much sleep. I’ll be tired in a normal sense, maybe, but it doesn’t matter because I feel like there’s something important I’m supposed to be doing. Like if I slow down, something is going to get overlooked.\n\nBut then if my husband tells me I need to rest, or if a nurse at work tells me to slow down or leave something alone, I get really irritated. I snap. I hear myself doing it sometimes, but in the moment it feels like they’re blocking me from doing something important, like they don’t understand the urgency. It’s not just being annoyed — it feels like they’re getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about the wound-care charts would change if an independent auditor found no evidence of wrongdoing?\n\nPatient: I mean… if it was truly independent, I would have to hear what they said. I’m not trying to be unreasonable. If someone came in from the outside and went through the wound-care charts carefully and could show, not just say, but show that there was no wrongdoing, I would sit there and look at it.\n\nBut I guess the hard part is that “independent” doesn’t always feel independent to me. People say that word like it automatically means nobody has a reason to protect management, but I’ve seen how things get smoothed over. I know what I saw in those charts. It’s the pattern — the timing of the changes, the late entries, the coding explanations that only come after I point something out. My supervisor keeps saying it’s just normal documentation problems, but it doesn’t feel normal when it keeps lining up like that.\n\nAnd honestly, once I caught it, I could tell they started watching what I write and what I flag. That changes everything, because then any audit could also be about containing it, making me look like I’m reading too much into it, instead of actually looking at what the charts are showing.\n\nSo… would it change my mind? Maybe a little, if I really trusted the person and they explained every piece clearly. But if they just came back with “no evidence of wrongdoing” and expected me to drop it, no. Because I know those charts are showing something management doesn’t want people to see. That’s not something I can just ignore because someone puts a clean report on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your interpretation of the patterns in the wound-care charts is incorrect, even if the voices are telling you something?\n\nPatient: I mean… I understand why you’re asking that, and I can say the words that, yes, technically anybody can misread something. I’m not saying I’m incapable of ever being wrong about a chart or a code or one late entry. I know paperwork gets messy. I’ve worked around enough nurses and documentation to know things don’t always line up perfectly.\n\nBut this doesn’t feel like just me interpreting one odd thing the wrong way. That’s the part I keep trying to explain. It’s the pattern. It’s how the wound-care charts change, and then when I bring it up, there’s suddenly an explanation ready — late entries, coding mistakes, normal corrections — but it doesn’t explain why it keeps happening in a way that points in the same direction. It’s like they want me to accept each piece separately, like, “Oh, this one is nothing, that one is nothing,” but when you put them together, it shows something management doesn’t want people to see.\n\nAnd even with the voices… I know you’re asking if maybe that could be affecting how I see it. I can understand that question. But I don’t feel like the charts only matter because of that. I saw the discrepancies. I wrote down what didn’t match. And after I started catching it, I could tell they were watching what I wrote and what I flagged. That’s not just a feeling floating in the air — it lines up with what happened after I noticed the pattern.\n\nSo… possible? I guess in some abstract way, yes. But honestly, no, I don’t think I’m wrong about this. I think those charts are showing something, and I think management knows it, and that’s why they keep trying to make it sound like routine documentation errors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any chance that your beliefs about the wound-care charts are not based in reality?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really think so, no. I know how that sounds, because you’re asking if there’s some chance I’ve built this up in my head or connected things that aren’t actually connected. I understand the question. But when I look at those wound-care charts, it isn’t like I’m inventing something out of nothing.\n\nThere is a pattern there. The entries change, or the explanations come after the fact, and then when I bring it up, my supervisor says it’s just late entries or coding mistakes or normal documentation issues. But it keeps happening in the same direction, over and over, and that’s what people don’t seem to want to acknowledge. One mistake is one thing. A bunch of them lining up that way is something else.\n\nAnd after I caught it, after I started writing things down and flagging what didn’t match, I could tell they were watching what I wrote. Not openly, not like someone saying, “We’re watching you,” but in the way people act when they know you saw something. The way my notes get questioned, the way they suddenly pay attention to what I’m documenting. It feels very clear to me that management doesn’t want anyone to see what those charts are showing.\n\nSo no… I don’t think it’s not based in reality. I think the reality is there, in the charts, and people keep trying to explain it away."
